The Pattern Complexity of the Sierpiński Triangle


Abstract
We give exact formulas for the number of distinct triangular patterns (or subtriangles) of a given size that occur in the Sierpiński Triangle.



Johan Nilsson

†
†
footnotetext:
MSC2010 classification:
05A15 Exact enumeration problems,
05B45 Tessellation and tiling problems,
52C20 Tilings in 2 dimensions.

1
Introduction

The Sierpiński triangle
[
5
]
is a well known fractal structure. It can be created by starting from an equilateral triangle, which is sub-divided into 4 equilateral triangles and where the central one is removed. The removal procedure is now repeated recursively on the tree remaining triangles. The Sierpiński triangle appears in many areas, e.g. the attractor in the
chaos game

[
2
]
, in Pascal’s triangle
[
6
, p. 80]
, or in Wolfram’s rule 90 in
[
6
, p. 25]
, just to mention a few. In the On-Line Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences OEIS
[
7
]
there are several entries concerning the Sierpiński triangle, such as
A047999
, and
A070886
.

The approach we will apply here, to create the Serpiński triangle, is to use the substitution rule
\mu
defined by

(1)

and where
is taken as seed. See Figure
1
for an illustration of the first iterations of
\mu
. Note that there are no filled downward oriented triangles with side length 1 in (
1
), (downwards meaning that precisely one corner is at the bottom. Similarly, if a triangle has precisely one corner at the top, we say that it is oriented upwards). We denote by
T
the limit structure obtained under iteration of
\mu
on
, and we call it the Sierpiński triangle.

In this paper we focus on the different triangular patterns (or subtriangles) that occur in
T
, and we prove the following theorem.

, generating the Sierpiński triangle, where

.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
A_{n}
be the number of unique upwards oriented triangular patterns of side length
n
that occur in the Sierpiński triangle
T
. Then

A_{n}=4n^{2}-6n+4

(2)

for
n\geq 1
. Similarly, let
A^{\prime}_{n}
be the number of downwards oriented triangular patterns of side length
n
that occur in
T
. Then
A^{\prime}_{1}=1
, and

A^{\prime}_{n}=n^{2}-3n+4

(3)

for
n\geq 2
.

The value given by
A^{\prime}_{n}
in Theorem
1.1
is also the maximal number of regions obtained when dividing the plane with
n-1
circles, see sequences
A014206
, and
A386480
in OEIS
[
7
]
, and also
[
8
]
. If there is a direct connection between this division of the plane and the patterns of
T
is at the moment not known to the author.

Example of similar results to Theorem
1.1
are Allouche’s result of the pattern complexity in paper-folding sequences
[
1
]
, Nilsson’s generalisation of the paper-folding structures into 2 dimensions
[
3
]
, and properties of the squiral tiling given in
[
4
]
.

The outline of this paper is as follows. In the next section we give definitions and provide some initial results. Thereafter, we turn to looking at properties of sets of patterns and give some tools for enumerating such sets. Succeeding this, in section
4
we give a list of recursions describing the size of sets of patterns, and in the final section we tie everything up and prove Theorem
1.1
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we give basis notations and definitions, we will state and prove a couple of some initial results.

\mu
in (

). We use the notation

(4)

See Figure
1
for a visualisation of the first
T_{n}
s. The limit of the sequence of the
T_{n}
s the Sierpiński triangle and we denote it by
T
, as also already mentioned in the introduction.

By the notion
pattern
we shall mean a triangular region without holes that occur somewhere in
T
. We also say that
T
is a pattern (an infinite one). Note that a we do not cut the triangles of unit side length to create a pattern. Hence, any finite pattern is an equilateral triangle. A pattern with
n
rows of unit triangles is said to be of size
n
. We consider two main kind of patterns, the patterns that have precisely one corner at the top and those with precisely one at the bottom. We say the former are upwards oriented and the latter downwards oriented.

The unit length triangles in a pattern
x
can be indexed via a pair
(r,c)
where
r
is the row counted from the top starting with 0, and
c
the column counted from the left starting with 0. We allow us to loosen the definition of patterns to also include an equilateral region where we cut off one unit length triangle at one or more corners. The unit length triangles in such a pattern are indexed in the same way as in a pattern with all corners uncut. That is, if we cut off the top triangle in an upwards oriented pattern
p
then the first row in
p
will have row-number 1. Note that we say that a pattern is of size
n
even if we have cut off a corner, see Figure
2
.

Proof.

We give a proof by induction on

in (

). The basis case,

, is direct from the assumption (

). Assume for induction that (

) holds for

.

For the induction step,

, take a pattern
a\in P(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 12.6 0 L 6.3 10.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

. Then there is a pattern
b\in P(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 12.6 0 L 6.3 10.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{

is a subpattern of

. By the induction assumption we have that

{}{}

. This implies

a\in P(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{

, and by Lemma

it follows that

3
Intersections

In this section we discuss properties of sets of patterns. Recall the definition of the particular sets of patterns given in (
6
). These sets can be split into subsets (not necessarily disjoint) depending on their pattern’s position in the underlying structure of super-tiles of size 2. Let us start by defining a set of indices (pair of integers) by

I:=\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,2),(2,2)\}.

For indices
(r,c)\in I
, and
n\geq 2
we introduce the following set of patterns

P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n):=\big\{\mu(x)[\alpha,r,c,n]:x\in P(\alpha,T,n)\big\},

(9)

where
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
is the type of pattern, see (
5
). The definition in (
9
) can be extend to further indices via

P_{r+2s,c+4t}(\alpha,T,n):=P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n),

(10)

for
s,t\in\mathbb{N}
. The above definitions lead to

P(\alpha,T,n)=\bigcup_{(r,c)\in I}P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n).

(11)

The sets on the right-hand side of (
11
) are not pairwise disjoint, as we will show in the following theorem, which is also the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
, and
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
. Then

\left|P(\alpha,T,n)\right|=-6+\sum_{(r,c)\in I}\left|P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n)\right|.

(12)

The proof of Theorem
3.1
is based on several lemmas, given below. In order to state the results of the lemmas we need to introduce a list of notation for special types of patterns.

represent a downwards oriented pattern of size
n
consisting of unfilled unit triangles, and at the marked side the upwards oriented unit triangles are filled.

The above notations describe the corners and sides in a pattern, and a pattern may consist of different types of corners; cut or uncut, marked or unmarked, and so on. With the help of the above definitions we can now give a string of lemmas leading up to the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

Let us consider the equality in (
13
). We prove it by induction on
n
. The basis cases
n=1,2
are seen via a straight forward enumeration. Assume (

) holds for

. For the induction step,
n=p+1
, we take a pattern

x\in P_{0,0}(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 12.6 0 L 6.3 10.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

.

\displaystyle=\left\{\hbox to23.16pt{\vbox to20.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

We have now given the lemmas needed to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

From the lemmas above, (Lemma
3.2
– Lemma
3.11
) we see that any of the sets
P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n)
contains the pattern with only unfilled unit-length triangles, meaning that this pattern is counted 4 times in the sum

\sum_{(r,c)\in I}|P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n)|,

(19)

that is, 3 times too many. Moreover, in each of the lemmas we see that there are precisely 3 intersections where 1 extra pattern is counted, besides the one with only unfilled triangles. Hence there is an additional over-count of 3 patterns in (
19
). This leads to the equality in (
12
).
∎

4
Recursions

The aim of this section is to give a list of recursion relations for the size of the sets
P_{r,c}(\alpha,T,n)
. We start by introducing the following short hand

.

\begin{split}A_{2n}&=-6+\sum_{(r,c)\in I}|P_{r,c}(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\begin{split}A^{\prime}_{2n}&=-6+\sum_{(r,c)\in I}|P_{r,c}(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 10.91 L 12.6 10.91 L 6.3 0 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},T,2n)|\\
&=-6+A^{\prime}_{n}+A^{\prime}_{n+1}+A^{\prime}_{n+1}+A^{\prime}_{n+1}.\end{split}

(30)

for a visualisation of the deduction of this recursion.

\displaystyle\begin{split}A^{\prime}_{2n+1}&=-6+\sum_{(r,c)\in I}|P_{r,c}(\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to8.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

5
Proof of Main Theorem

In this section we provide the final steps the proof of Theorem
1.1
. First, recall the definition of the quantities
A_{n}
,
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
, and
D_{n}
from (
20
). By the help of the recursions in the previous section we can prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
. Then

\begin{split}A_{n}&=C_{n+1},\\
B_{n}&=D_{n+1}.\end{split}

(32)

Proof.

We give a proof by induction. Let us consider the index
n
in the equalities in (
32
) depending on whether it is odd or even. The initial cases are directly seen from Table
1
. Assume for induction that the equalities in (
32
) hold for
n<2p
. Then, for the induction step, we have from (
22
), (
27
), and the induction assumption

\displaystyle A_{2p}-C_{2p+1}

\displaystyle=A_{p}+3B_{p+1}-C_{p+1}-2B_{p+1}-D_{p+2}=0,

and with the help of (
23
), and (
26
) we get

\displaystyle A_{2p+1}-C_{2p+2}

\displaystyle=3A_{p+1}+D_{p+2}-A_{p+1}-2C_{p+2}-D_{p+2}=0.

In the same way we obtain

\displaystyle B_{2p}-D_{2p+1}

\displaystyle=A_{p}+2C_{p+1}+B_{p+1}-3C_{p+1}-D_{p+2}=0,

and

\displaystyle B_{2p+1}-D_{2p+2}

\displaystyle=A_{p+1}+2B_{p+1}+D_{p+2}-A_{p+1}-3D_{p+2}=0,

which complete the induction and the proof.
∎

By the help of Lemma
5.1
and the recursions from the previous section, we obtain

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{ \ }l@{ \ }l@{ \ }l@{}l@{ \ }l@{ \ }l@{}l}A_{2n}&=-6&+&&A_{n}&+&3&B_{n+1},\\
A_{2n+1}&=-6&+&3&A_{n+1}&+&&B_{n+1},\\
B_{2n}&=-6&+&3&A_{n}&+&&B_{n+1},\\
B_{2n+1}&=-6&+&&A_{n+1}&+&3&B_{n+1}.\end{array}\right.

(33)

Recall also the recursions for
A^{\prime}_{n}
from (
30
) and
31

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{ \ }l@{ \ }l@{ \ }l@{}l@{ \ }l@{ \ }l@{}l}A^{\prime}_{2n}&=-6&+&&A^{\prime}_{n}&+&3&A^{\prime}_{n+1},\\
A^{\prime}_{2n+1}&=-6&+&3&A^{\prime}_{n+1}&+&&A^{\prime}_{n+2}.\end{array}\right.

(34)

The initial values for (
33
) and (
34
) are given in Table
1
and Table
2
respectively. The last step is now to give the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

To prove (
2
), we give simultaneously an explicit formula for
B_{n}
from (
33
). That is, we claim the following

\displaystyle A_{n}

\displaystyle=4n^{2}-6n+4,

(35)

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle=4n^{2}-10n+8.

(36)

We give a proof of the above claim by induction on
n
. The initial cases are directly from Table
1
. Assume for induction that (
35
) and (
36
) hold for
n<2p
. For the induction step we have by (
33
), (
35
), (
36
), and the induction assumption

\displaystyle A_{2p}

\displaystyle-\big(4(2p)^{2}-6(2p)+4\big)

\displaystyle=-6+A_{p}+3B_{p+1}-\big(4(2p)^{2}-6(2p)+4\big)

\displaystyle=-6+\big(4p^{2}-6p+4\big)+3\big(4(p+1)^{2}-10(p+1)+8\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\big(4(2p)^{2}-6(2p)+4\big)

\displaystyle=0.

The remaining cases for
A_{2p+1}
,
B_{2p}
, and
B_{2p+1}
follow in the same way.

Similarly, the formula for
A^{\prime}_{n}
from (
3
) is easily verified for small
n
from Table
2
. Assume for induction that (
3
) holds for
n<2p
. Then, in the induction step, we have with the help of (
34
)

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{2p}

\displaystyle-\big((2p)^{2}-3(2p)+4\big)

\displaystyle=-6+A^{\prime}_{p}+3A^{\prime}_{p+1}-\big((2p)^{2}-3(2p)+4\big)

\displaystyle=-6+\big(p^{2}-3p+4\big)+3\big((p+1)^{2}-3(p+1)+4\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\big((2p)^{2}-3(2p)+4\big)

\displaystyle=0.

The case for
n=2p+1
follows analogously.
∎

Let us close the paper with a small remark and question; from the formulas for
A_{n}
,
B_{n}
, and
A^{\prime}_{n}
from (
2
), (
36
), and (
3
) we see that there is a neat connection between the number of upward- and downward oriented triangular patterns, namely

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{ \ }l}A^{\prime}_{2n}&=A_{n},\\
A^{\prime}_{2n-1}&=B_{n},\\
\end{array}\right.

(37)

for
n\geq 2
. It would be interesting to see and find out if there is a direct geometrical or combinatorial argument leading to the connection in (
37
), or if it is just a numerical coincidence.
